QUESTION PRESENTED 


Whether the United States, after having accepted con- 
sideration from a government contractor, is required to 
return that consideration (i.e., pay restitution) when sub- 
sequent government action bars the United States from 
performing its contractual obligations?

In THE 


Supreme Court of the Wuited States 


No. 99- 


MARATHON OIL COMPANY, 


. Petitioner, 


UNITED STATES OF AMERICA,

On Petition for Writ of Certiorari to the

for the Federal Circuit 


PETITION FOR WRIT OF CERTIORARI 


Petitioner Marathon Oil Company (“Marathon Oil”) 
respectfully petitions for a writ of certiorari to review the 
judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Federal Circuit in this case. 


OPINIONS BELOW 


The superseding opinion of the Court of Federal Claims 
(App. 45a-99a) granting petitioner’s motion for partial 
summary judgment as to liability is reported at 35 Fed. 
Cl. 309. The judgment of the Court of Federal Claims 
in favor of petitioner (App. 44a) is unreported. The 
original opinion of the United States Court of Appeals for 
the Federal Circuit (App. 25a-43a) reversing the judg- 
ment of the Court of Federal Claims was entered on 
October 15, 1998, and is reported at 158 F.3d 1253. 


2 


The court of appeals granted panel rehearing and entered 
a revised order and opinion (App. 1a-24a) on May 13, 
1999, which is reported at 177 F.3d 1331. 


JURISDICTION 


The court of appeals entered its original decision on 
October 15, 1998. On November 25, 1998, petitioner 
timely filed a petition for rehearing and suggestion for 
rehearing en banc. The court granted panel rehearing and 
entered a revised judgment on May 13, 1999. This 
Court has jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1254(1). 


STATUTES INVOLVED 


The statutes involved in this case are (1) the Outer 
Continental Shelf Lands Act of 1953, ch. 345, 67 Stat. 
462 (codified as amended at 43 U.S.C. § 1331 ef seq.), 
(2) the Outer Banks Protection Act, Pub. L. No. 101- 
380, § 6003, 104 Stat. 484, 555-58 (1990), and (3) the 
Coastal Zone Management Act, 16 U.S.C. § 1456, which 
are reproduced in pertinent part in the appendix at 
100a-158a, 159a-165a, and 166a-173a, respectively. 


STATEMENT OF THE CASE 


Judge Newman's dissent from the Federal Circuit's de- 
cision on rehearing summarizes the question presented in 
this case. 


This is a contract case. The Court of Federal Claims, 
determining that the contract between [p. .‘tioner] 
Marathon [Oil Company] and the United States was 
made impossible of performance by governmental 
action, held that Marathon [and its partner] [are] 
entitled to recover what [they] paid the government 
for the now-barred contract rights, $156,000,000 paid 
up-front. 


The legal issue is not complex. When one party to a 
contract prevents the other party from achieving the 


3 


benefit for which it paid, the party who prevents the 
performance can not retain the consideration it ac- 
cepted for the performance. . . . Marathon does not 
challenge the government’s change of policy [that 
barred it from exercising its contract rights]; nor does 
Marathon seek damages, or specific performance, or 
other interesting things. Marathon just wants its 
money back. 


The Court of Federal Claims, applying simple con- 
tract principles, ordered the government to return 
this payment. My colleagues now authorize the gov- 
ernment to keep it. That is not the law, and it is not 
responsible government dealing. 


App. 19a-20a (footnote omitted). Judge Newman's anal- 
ysis is correct and provides a complete rationale for fur- 
ther review by this Court. 


1. Outer Continental Shelf Lands Leasing Scheme 


The Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act of 1953 
(“OCSLA”), ch. 345, 67 Stat. 462 (codified as amended 
at 43 U.S.C. § 1331 et seq.), establishes federal jurisdic- 
tion over the seabed and subsoil of the continental sheif 
lying more than three miles from the coast of the United 
States (“OCS lands”). See 43 U.S.C. § 1333(a). The 
OCSLA further authorizes the Secretary of the Interior 
(“the Secretary”) to sell leases to private parties to 
explore for and develop oil and gas deposits on those 
lands. See id. § 1337. Those leases “entitle the lessee 
to explore, develop, and produce the oil and gas contained 
within the lease area,” subject only to the lessee’s duc 
diligence and compliance with the OCSLA and regulations 
promulgated thereunder. Jd. § 1337(b) (4). 


Under the OCSLA, leases for tracts of OCS land are 
sold by the Department of the Interior (“DOI”) through 


4 


a competitive bidding process. The winning bidders in 
this process are required to pay large, up-front, cash 
“bonuses” as well as annual lease payments for their 
leases and—if production is ever achieved—royalties on 
any oil or gas produced. See Watt v. Energy Action 
Educ. Found., 454 U.S. 151 (1981) (upholding DOI’s 
up-front, cash “bonus” system). Since OCSLA’s enact- 
ment in 1953, over 19.000 OCS leases have been sold and 
successful bidders have paid more than $60.7 billion in 
"bonuses” to the United States for the right to explore 
their tracts and develop any oil and gas deposits they 
find. See Minerals Management Serv., U.S. Dep’t of the 
Interior, Mineral Revenues 1998, at 51 (prelim. ed. 
1999). 


When a lease tract is not adjacent to known deposits of 
oil or gas, the first step in expiviiing an OCS lease is the 
preparation and submission to the Secretary of a plan of 
exploration (“POE”). See 43 U.S.C. § 1340(b), (c)(1). 
A POE must set forth, in detail, a schedule of planned 
exploration activities, a description of the equipment to 
be used, the location of each exploratory well to be 
drilled, and other pertinent information. See id. § 1340 
(c)(3). Following submission of a POE, the Secretary 
is required to determine whether the POE is “consistent” 
with the OCSLA and the regulations promulgated there- 
under. Id. § 1340(c)(1). The Secretary may require 
modifications of a POE to achieve such “consistency,” 
and upon determining that the POE is “consistent” with 
the OCSLA, “shall” approve the POE. Id. (emphasis 
added). 


The OCSLA places a strict time limit for action by the 
Secretary with respect to a POE that is “consistent” with 
the OCSLA. Under the OCSLA’s express terms, “[t]he 
Secretary shall approve such plan, as submitted or modi- 


5 


fied, within thirty days of its submission,” id. (emphasis 
added), unless (A) the proposed exploratory activities 
would probably cause serious harm or damage to, inter 
alia, life (including fish and other aquatic life) or the 
environment and (B) those activities “cannot be modified 
to avoid” such harm or damage, id. ( emphasis added) 
(incorporating by reference id. § 1334(a)(2)(A)(i)). If 
the Secretary disapproves a POE that is “consistent” with 
the OCSLA under the foregoing provision, he may cancel 
the lease, entitling the lessee to compensation. Jd. § 1340 


(c)(1). 


After a POE is approved, the next step in the process is 
for the lessee to obtain the necessary permits to begin 
exploratory drilling. Jd. § 1337(d). In order to obtain a 
federal drilling permit, a lessee whose activities will affect 
land or water uses in the coastal zone of a State with a 
federally approved coastal zone management program 
must, under the Coastal Zone Management Act, 16 
U.S.C. § 1456(c)(3)(B) (“CZMA”), certify that its pro- 
posed activities comply with the State’s program. Under 
the OCSLA, the Secretary may not issue a drilling permit 
unless the State concurs, id. § 1456(c)(3)(B)(i), or is 
presumed to concur with that certification, id. § 1456(c) 
(3)(B) (ii), or the State’s objection is overridden by the 
Secretary of Commerce, id. § 1456(c)(3)(B) (iii). See 
43 U.S.C. § 1340(c)(2). 


A lessee’s failure to obtain a State’s CZMA concur- 
rence is not a ground under the OCSLA for withholding 
approval of an otherwise approvable POE. Although the 
OCSLA expressly prohibits the Secretary from granting a 
drilling license or permitting exploratory activity without 
a State’s CZMA concurrence (or a federal override of its 
objection), it does not restrain him from taking the first 


.step—approving a POE that complies with the OCSLA— 


6 


even in the face of a State’s CZMA objection. Jd. Indeed, 
during the relevant period, the DOI’s own regulations made 
clear that a POE complying with the OCSLA was approv- 
able notwithstanding a State’s CZMA objection, but that 
applications for drilling permits needed to commence ex- 
ploratory activities could not be approved without the 
State’s CZMA concurrence. Compare 30 C.F.R. § 250.33 
(i) (1993) with id. § 250.33(p). 


If a lessee’s initial exploration of its tract proves suc- 
cessful, it may (and often will) pursue additional ex- 
ploratory activities by preparing and submitting additional 
POEs. For example, when a first exploratory well finds 
oil or gas, the next step is often to drill additional wells 
to locate the boundaries of the deposit. Finally, in order 
to extract any oil or gas found, the lessee must prepare 
and submit a development and production plan (“POD”). 
See 43 U.S.C. § 1351(a)(1). 


2. Petitioner’s Leases 


In August 1981, petitioner purchased one-third undi- 
vided lease interests (along with Mobil Oil Exploration 
and Producing Southeast, Inc. (“Mobil”) and Amerada 
Hess Corporation) in five OCS tracts off the coast of 
North Carolina. In exchange for the right to “explore, 
develop and produce the oil and gas contained within” 
these tracts. 43 U.S.C. § 1337(b)(4), the lessees (in- 
cluding petitioner) each paid more than $78 million to 
the United States in up-front, cash “bonuses.” App. 7a. 
The lessees each subsequently paid a total of more than 
$264,000 in annual lease payments to the United States 
during the leases’ ten-year primary term. /d. 


Each of the lease agreements between petitioner and the 
United States was a non-negotiable, standard-form con- 


7 


tract prepared by the DOI. See App. 174a-192a (sample 
OCS lease). Under the leases, petitioner acquired “the 
exclusive right and privilege to drill for, develop, and 
produce oil and gas resources, except helium gas” on its 
OCS tracts. Jd. at 175a (§ 2).1 As the Court of Federal 
Claims found, the United States obligated itself to “timely 
and fairly consider—not necessarily approve, but at least 
consider—exploration plans properly submitted.” /d. at 
76a. “Otherwise,” the court concluded, “the bargain be- 
tween the [United States] and [petitioner] would be illu- 
sory and would permit [the government’s] unilateral for- 
feiture of [petitioner’s] lease bonuses with impunity.” 
Id. at 57a; see also id. at 76a. 


In 1989, petitioner, the co-lessees of its OCS tracts, 
and owners of other adjacent OCS leases organized their 
tracts into a single exploration unit (the “Manteo Unit”) 
and proposed to drill a single exploratory well approxi- 
mately 45 miles east of Cape Hateras.? After the State of 
North Carolina raised initial environmental objections to 
the plan, Mobil (the operator of the Manteo Unit) en- 
tered into a memorandum of understanding with the DOT 
and the State on July 14, 1989. In this memorandum, 
Mobil agreed to produce a draft POE for DOI’s and the 
State’s review and to delay submitting a final POE until 
after DOI had completed a special environmental report 
assessing the environmental impacts of the proposed ex- 
ploration of the Manteo Unit. App. 7a. 


1 The lease also reiterated the pre-existing requirement of the 
OCSLA that, in exploring and developing its OCS tracts, “shall 
comply with all regulations and orders relating to exploration, 
development, and production.” App. 178a (§ 10). 


2 Four of petitioner’s five OCS tracts were included in the 
Manteo Unit. The fifth—Currituck Sound Block No. 777—was not. 


On June 1, 1990, the DOT issued a three-volume, 2000- 
page special environmental report. Based on this report, 
which one DOI official agreed was “the most extensive 
and intensive environmental examination that has ever 
been afforded an exploration well in the OCS program,” 
J.A. 949-50, the DOI determined that the proposed ex- 
ploration of the Manteo Unit would not result in any 
significant environmental impacts—either offshore or to 
the North Carolina coastal zone—and that the Manteo 
Unit's POE was consistent with the OCSLA and “approv- 
able in all respects.” App. 194a. 


3. Outer Banks Protection Act 


On August 18, 1990—before the Manteo Unit's final 
POE was submitted to the Secretary—the United States 
enacted the Outer Banks Protection Act (“OBPA”) as 
part of the comprehensive Oil Pollution Act of 1990. See 
Pub. L. No. 101-380, § 6003, 104 Stat. 484, 555-58 
(1990). This Act—without amending the OCSLA— 
expressly prohibited the United States from fulfilling its 
contractual obligations under petitioner’s North Carolina 
OCS leases. By its terms, the OBPA imposed an open- 
ended “moratorium.” App. 7a, on the approval of any 
exploration or development of North Carolina OCS lands 
by prohibiting the Secretary from: 


(A) conduct[ing] a lease sale; 

(B) issufing] any new leases; 

(C) approv[ing] any exploration plan; 

(D) approv[ing] any development and production 
plan; 

(E) approv[ing] any application for permit to 
drill; or 

(F) permit[ing] any drilling, 


SO ee rene aww 


9 


for oil or gas under the Outer Continental Shelf 
Land Act on any lands of the Outer Continental 
Shelf offshore North Carolina. 


OBPA § 6003(c)(1). This moratorium extended for at 
least 13 months, until Ottober 1, 1991, and thereafter 
until the forty-fifth day of continuous congressional ses- 
sion after the Secretary submitted a written report to 
Congress certifying that the available information was 
sufficient to enable him to carry out his responsibilities 
under the OCSLA. /d. § 6003(c)(3)(A). The OBPA 
further mandated that the Secretary’s report could be 
made only after he had considered the findings and rec- 
ommendations of an Environmental Sciences Review 
Panel (“Review Panel”), which the OBPA created. /d. 
§ 6003 (c) (3) (A) (it). 


The OBPA’s purpose is apparent from its history. In 
1988 and 1989, Congress enacted similar “moratoria” by 
prohibiting the DOI from expending any funds to ap- 
prove any exploration of OCS tracts offshore of both 
Florida and Alaska. See Pub. L. 100-446, $110, 102 
Stat. 1774, 1801 (1988) (Florida); Pub. L. 101-121, 
§ 111, 103 Stat. 701, 720 (1989) (North Aleutian 
Basin). In June 1990, in response to environmental con- 
cerns raised by several States, President Bush instructed 
the DOI to cancel planned auctions of OCS leases off- 
shore of California and Florida, to “[b]Jegin cancellation 
of existing leases off Florida,” and to explore a “joint 
federal-state buy-back” of the Florida OCS leases. J.A. 
988-89. 


The State of North Carolina, and members of its con- 
gressional delegation, strenuously protested the omission 
of OCS tracts off its coast from these bans on explora- 
tion. In July 1990, Representative Jones of North Caro- 
lina introduced what would become the OBPA as an 


10 


amendment to the Oil Pollution Act of 1990. Repre- 
sentative Jones candidly explained the purpose of the 
OBPA: “{I]n his offshore moratoria proposal, the Presi- 
dent has forgotten about North Carolina. Well, I have 
not.” 136 Cong. Rec. 22,275 (1990).% 


4. Application Of The OBPA 


On August 20, 1990—two days after the enactment 
of the OBPA—Mobil, on behalf of the Manteo Unit, sub- 
mitted its final POE to drill a single exploratory well on 
the North Carolina OCS lands leased by the Unit. More 
than a month later, on September 28, 1990, DOI found 
that the Manteo Unit’s POE complied with all DOI regu- 
lations and would have no significant impact on the en- 
vironment. The DOI reported to the Governor of North 
Carolina as follows: 


The [Manteo Unit] Plan, together with its supporting 
information, is deemed to be approvable in all re- 
spects. The Outer Banks Protection Act (OBPA) of 
1990, however, prohibits the approval of any Ex- 
ploration Plan at this time. Under Section 11(c) of 
the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act [43 U.S.C. 
§ 1337(c)], we are required to approve an Explora- 
tion Plan unless it is inconsistent with applicable law 
or because it would result in serious harm to the 
environment. Because we have found that [the 
Manteo Unit’s] Plan fully complies with the law and 
will have only negligible effect on the environment, 


3 Representative Rose of North Carolina expressed similar views: 


Governor Martin [of North Carolina] . . . was horrified when 
the President stopped offshore drilling off the coast of .. . 
Florida and left North Carolina out. And so he. . . wrote to 
Mr. Jones, and Mr. Jones did exactly what [Governor Martin] 
asked him to do, he put [the OBPA] in the legislation. 


136 Cong. Rec. 22,277 (1990). 


——— 


11 


we are not authorized to disapprove the Plan or 
require its modification as you suggest. 

However, because we are currently prohibited from 
approving it, the Plan will remain on file until the 
requirements of the OBPA are met. Our determina- 
tions concerning this Plan will be re-evaluated at 
that time. 


App. 194a. Thus, although the DOI found the Manteo 
Unit’s POE was “approvable in all respects,” the Secre- 
tary nonetheless withheld his approval in order to comply 
with the OBPA and suspended petitioner’s OCS leases— 
including the one that was not part of the Manteo Unit— 
as of August 18, 1990, the OBPA’s effective date.* 


Significantly, the OBPA’s ban did not extend only to 
the specific exploratory activities proposed in the Manteo 
Unit's pending POE. By its terms, the OBPA prohibited 
the Secretary from approving any POE, permitting any 
drilling, or approving any POD for any North Carolina 
OCS tract, even if a lessee’s application complied in every 
respect with the OCSLA and was supported (rather than 
opposed) by the State. See OBPA § 6003(c)(1). In 
short, as the Court of Federal Claims found, see App. 
75a, the OBPA foreclosed petitioner from pursuing any 
of the rights it had purchased from the United States to 
explore aad develop its North Carolina OCS tracts. 


On November 19, 1990—even though the DOI had 
concluded two months earlier that the Manteo Unit's 


4 DOI lifted this suspension on June 8, 1992, after the Secretary 
certified that he had sufficient information to perform his responsi- 
bilities under the OCSLA with respect to the Manteo Unit’s POF, 
see infra at 12. At Mobil’s request, the suspension was reinstated 
on October 5, 1992, and remained in effect until November 9, 1994, 
after two of the additional studies recommended by the Review 
Panel were completed and the Secretary of Commerce had denied 
the Manteo Unit's appeals of North Carolina’s CZMA objections, 
see infra at 12 & n.5. 


12 


planned exploratory activities complied in all respects with 
the OCSLA and would not have any significant impact 
on the environment—the State of North Carolina ob- 
jected to the Manteo Unit's certification that its POE com- 
plied with the CZMA. App. 8a. Echoing the OBPA’s 
findings, North Carolina’s «byection was based principally 
on its assertion that the available information was insuffi- 
cient to assess the impact on the North Carolina coastal 
zone. The Manteo Unit promptly appealed the State’s 
CZMA objection to its POE to the Secretary of Com- 
merce. See infra at 12 n.5. 


In January 1992—almost a year after the deadline set 
by the OBPA—the Review Panel established by the OBPA 
belatedly submitted its report to the Secretary. After re- 
viewing this report, the Secretary certified to Congress on 
April 2, 1992, that “sufficient information presently exists 
to allow me to exercise my responsibilities under the 
OCSLA .. . and consider approval of the exploration 
activities currently proposed to take place offshore North 
Carolina (i.e., the first Manteo exploratory well).” App. 
202a. Nevertheless, the Secretary refused to approve the 
Manteo Unit’s pending POE or permit the drilling of the 
single proposed exploratory well until the completion of 
two studies that had been recommended by the Review 
Panel. Jd. Although these studies were completed in July 
1994, the Secretary ultimately never approved—or disap- 
proved—the Manteo Unit’s POE.® 


As the government conceded below, by its express 
terms, the OBPA completely barred all activities required 
for petitioner and its partners to exploit their North 
Carolina OCS tracts from its enactment in 1990 until its 


5On September 2, 1994, the Secretary of Commerce declined to

Commerce’s decision was based in large part on the OBPA and 
the recommendations of the Review Panel it had created. 


13 


repeal in 1996, see Pub. L. No. 104-134, § 109, 110 
Stat. 1321, 1321-177 (1996)*—almost four years after 
petitioner filed this suit. The only OBPA certification the 
Secretary ever made to Congress extended only to the 
single exploratory well proposed in the Manteo Unit's 
POE, which the Secretary nonetheless declined to approve. 
In fact, the Secretary specifically advised Congress in 
April 1992 that further studies would be required for him 
to make the OBPA certification for additional (or differ- 
ent) wells, POEs, and PODs, and that those studies 
“w[ould] not begin in earnest prior to reviewing the initial 
exploration drilling.” App. 202a. Accordingly, even if the 
Manteo Unit’s POE had been approved and its single ex- 
ploratory well allowed to proceed, the OBPA continued— 
even after the Secretary’s April 1992 certification—as a 
bar to all (i) subsequent exploratory plans (such as those 
four additional wells to determine the boundaries of any 
deposits found by the first well), (ii) different explora- 
tory activities, and (iii) the ultimate production and de- 
velopment of any oil or gas deposits found. See id. at 75a. 


5. Proceedings Below 


In October 1992, Marathon Oil, along with the other 
owners of leases for North Carolina OCS tracts, joined a 
lawsuit filed by Conoco, Inc. against the United States for 
breach of the leases it had entered into for, inter alia, OCS 
tracts off the coast of North Carolina. In that suit. peti- 
tioner contended that the enactment and enforcement of 
the OBPA had materially breached its OCS leases by pre- 
venting the Secretary from performing his contractual 
duties. As the Court of Federal Claims explained, peti- 


6 As indicated by its legislative history, “[t)}he repeal . . . [wals 
not intended to excuse the United States from .. . liabilitfy), if 
any, it has incurred to date nor otherwise affect pending litigation.” 
141 Cong. Rec. H9443 (daily ed. Sept. 21, 1995). 


14 


tioner “d{id] not claim that [the government's] failure to 
approve [the Manteo Unit's] POEs and grant the neces- 
sary permits breached [its] lease contracts, but only that 
{the government's] failure at least to fairly consider their 
plans was a material breach.” App. 57a. Accordingly, 
petitioner sought restitution of the up-front, cash “bonuses” 
it paid, as well as its annual lease payments. 


On April 1, 1996, the Court of Federal Claims granted 
partial summary judgment for petitioner as to liability, 
holding that the United States’ “failure [to carry out its 
contractual obligations], under the restrictions adopted 
pursuant to the OBPA, was a material breach of the 
leases.” App. 84a. The court began by rejecting the United 
States’ principal claim that the text of the leases—which 
made them subject to the OCSLA, “ ‘all regulations . . . in 
existence upon the Effective Date of th[ese] lease[s]; .. . 
and all other applicable statutes and regulations,’” id. at 
52a, 75a (emphasis 2dded) (§ 1)—made petitioner’s con- 
tractual rights expressly subject to all future statutes en- 
acted by the United States, including one, such as the 
OBPA. which was both unforeseeable and effectively ex- 
tinguished those rights. See id. at 68a-69a. As the court 
concluded, “the lease contracts here at issue cannot be 
construed to allow for such drastic unilateral interference 
by the government, through passage of highly restrictive 
legislation, the OBPA, with the lessees’ bargained for 
rights of exploration.” Id. at 67a. 


The court next held that the United States’ enactment 
and implementation of the OBPA constituted a “total 
breach” of its lease contract with petitioner. 


[U]nder the lease agreements, defendant was con- 
tractually obligated to consider facially compliant 
POEs promptly and fairly. Any contrary interpre- 
tation would render the bargain illusory and subject 
to unilateral forfeiture. Clearly, the OBPA imposed 


RR Oe te 


15 


severe, burdensome, new conditions upon the DOT's 
obligation under OCSLA to approve POEs offshore 
North Carolina, which were not contemplated by the 
parties when the leases were executed. Therefore, the 
court also finds that compliance with the OBPA 
compelled governmental breach by non-performance 
accompanied by an anticipatory repudiation thereby 
giving rise to “total breach.” 


App. 76a.” On July 24, 1997, the Court of Federal Claims 
awarded Marathon Oil restitution of the up-front, cash 
“bonuses” it had paid for its North Carolina OCS leases 
and entered judgment in its favor in the amount of 
$78,242,368.59. Id. at 44a. 


On October 15, 1998, a divided panel of the Federal 
Circuit reversed. Judge Plager, joined by Judge Schall, 
wrote the majority opinion. Judge Newman dissented. 
Petitioner timely filed a petition for rehearing and sugges- 
tion for rehearing en banc. Thereafter, five members of 
the court of appeals, including Judge Schall, recused 
themselves, presumably as a result of Mobil’s merger with 
Exxon Corporation, which was announced in December 
1998. Judge Schall was replaced by Judge Rader. On 
May 13, 1999, without hearing re-argument, the newly- 
constituted panel issued a revised opinion again reversing 
the judgment of the Court of Federal Claims, again by a 
divided vote. 


7 Relving on Winstar Corp. v. United States, 64 F.3d 1531 (Fed. 
Cir. 1995) (en banc), aff'd, 518 U.S. 839 (1996), the court also 
rejected the government’s arguments that it was immune from 
liability under the sovereign acts and unmistakability doctrines, 
finding that the OBPA “was not an act of public, general appli- 
cability,” App. 96a, but was instead “narrowly tailored to target 
the specific contracts and sought to abate the accompanying rights 
here at issue,” id. at 91a. The government abandoned these argu- 
ments on appeal following this Court’s decision in United States vy. 
Winstar Corp., 518 U.S. 839 (1996). 


16 


The majority opinion, again authored by Judge Plager, 
first agreed with the Court of Federal Claims that peti- 
tioner’s OCS leases were not, by their terms, subject to 
modification by subsequent legislation such as the-OBPA. 
App. 12a. The court of appeals rejected the lower court’s 
conclusion that the OBPA had compelled the government 
to breach the leases, however, because, in the majority’s 
view, the OBPA was not the “real reason” for DOI’s 
failure to approve the Manteo Unit's POE. 


Whatever restraints on secretarial action were im- 
posed by the OBPA essentially had no effect upon 
these OCS leases because exploration could not pro- 
ceed without North Carolina’s concurrence in the 
POE’s CZMA consistency certification or the over- 
ride provided by law. .. . [E]ven if the Secretary 
had felt constrained in acting on the application by 
the provisions of the OBPA, the law is clear that had 
he been free to act, he could not have issued any 
permits for exploration so long as North Carolina’s 
objections remained in force. 


Id. at 14a-15a (citation omitted). The court then side- 
stepped petitioner’s argument that the State’s CZMA ob- 
jections were not independent of the OBPA and could 
have been overridden by the Secretary of Commerce, by 
characterizing petitioner’s argument as a challenge to the 
Secretary’s failure to act, an issue “not before us on 
appeal.” /d. at 16a. 


Finally, the majority turned to petitioner’s claims for 
restitution. Although the majority acknowledged that 
petitioner’s rights to explore and develop its OCS tracts 
had been frustrated, the court denied petitioner’s restitu- 
tion of the up-front “bonuses” it had paid on the ground 
that there was 


no principaled distinction between a lessee whose 
hopes for large rewards from such a lease are frus- 


17 


trated by a failure to obtain the necessary permits for 
exploration, and those of a lessee whose hopes are 
drowned by impossible weather, equipment failures, 
financial setbacks, or any number of other circum- 
stances beyond the lessee’s control. In either case, the 
lessee contracted for the exclusive opportunity to ex- 
plore in a certain area; the inability of a lessee to ex- 
plore, if not attributable to the Government, does not 
create an entitlement to any refund of the considera- 
tion paid to obtain the lease. 


Id. at 18a. 


Judge Newman dissented. In Judge Newman's view, 
this case presented a simple issue: “[W]hether the govern- 
ment shall deal fairly and legally with its contracting part- 
ners.” App. 21a. Petitioner and the government had 
entered into a contract under which petitioner had pur- 
chased an entitlement to explore its tracts, which was later 
eliminated by the enactment of the OBPA. 7d. at 20a. 
As Judge Newman explained: 


While it was the objection of North Carolina that led 
to the prohibition of exploration of the Outer Banks. 
first by state refusal to certify the exploration, then 
by [Congress’s] enactment of the Outer Banks Pro- 
tection Act, and then by the refusal of the Secretary 
of Commerce to override the state’s objection, this 
does not justify the federal government’s confiscation 
of Marathon’s payment for a contract entered into 
before any such objection arose. 


Id. at 20a-21a. The post-contract events that made it im- 
possible for the government to perform its promise to 
“timely and fairly consider’ exploration plans that were 
properly submitted,” id. at 21a, “were all outside the con- 
trol of Marathon,” id. at 22a. Applying both “hornbook 
law,” id., and a “wealth of precedent,” id. at 23a, Judge 


18 


Newman concluded that the “withdrawal of [petitioner’s] 
federal right,” id., required “return of the federal con- 
sideration,” id. As Judge Newman explained, any other 
result would relieve the government of its duty to “deal 
fairly and legally with its contracting partners.” /d. at 21a. 


REASONS FOR GRANTING THE PETITION 


I. THE DECISION BELOW CONFLICTS WITH THIS 
COURT'S PRECEDENTS HOLDING THAT GOV- 
ERNMENT CONTRACTS ARE GOVERNED BY 
THE LAW APPLICABLE TO PRIVATE CON- 
TRACTS. 


In this case, the Federal Circuit incorrectly created a 
rule of law excepting the United States from the normal 
contract principles that (1) a party whose performance 
is made impossible, either by its own acts or through 
supervening events, “is not privileged to keep something 
for nothing. and restitution is an available remedy against 
fit].” 5S Arthur Linton Corbin. Corbin on Contracts 
§ 1102, at 549 (1964) and (2) a party who makes its 
own performance of its contractual obligations impossible 
is in breach. e.¢., United States v. Winstar Corn., 518 
U.S. 839, 870 (1996). Tenoring these rules, the majority 
opinion of the Federal Circuit held that. having accepted 
$78 million from petitioner in exchange for the contractual 
right to explore its OCS tracts. the United States was 
nonetheless entitled to “keep something for nothing” when 
subsequent events. including subsequent federal legisla- 
tion. made its performance impossible and extinguished 
petitioner’s bargained-for rights. 


The Federal Circuit’s decision is in direct conflict with 
well-established contract law and—absent intervention bv 
this Court—will upset the long-settled expectation that 


ee a 


19 


“(t]he United States does business on business terms,” 
United States v. National Exch. Bank, 270 U.S. 527, 534 
(1926). The Federal Circuit has exclusive jurisdiction 
over appeals from the Court of Federal Claims, see 28 
U.S.C. § 1295(a)(3), which is the only court to which 
the owners of OCS leases and other government con- 
tractors may bring contract actions against the federal 
government, see id. §$ 1346(a)(2), 1491. There accord- 
ingly will rarely, if ever, be a conflict among the Circuits 
regarding issues raised in contract disputes with the United 
States. However, as this Court has noted, the rules that 
the Federal Circuit applies are “a matter of special im- 
portance to the entire Nation.” Cardinal Chem. Co. v. 
Morton Int'l, Inc., 508 U.S. 83, 89 (1993). This Court 
has not in the past hesitated to grant review in cases 
involving issues of recurring importance to the relation- 
ship between the federal government and private litigants. 
See, e.g., Gardner v. Brown, 5 F.3d 1456 (Fed. Cir. 
1993), cert. granted, 511 U.S. 1017 (1994). In fact, 
in light of the overriding national importance of the law 
governing the government's contracts with private parties, 
this Court has in recent years twice granted certiorari— 
in the absence of a circuit split—in order to review deci- 
sions of the Federal Circuit in government contract cases. 
See Hercules Inc. v. United States, 24 F.3d 188 (Fed. 
Cir. 1994), cert. granted, 514 U.S. 1049 (1995); Winstar 
Corp. v. United States, 64 F.3d 1531 (Fed. Cir. 1995), 
cert. granted, 516 U.S. 187 (1996).8 


1. Three years ago, this Court reaffirmed the long- 
standing principle that “‘[w]hen the United States enters 


8 This Court has also twice before granted certiorari, in the 
absence of a circuit split, to correct misconstructions of the OCSLA 
and other statutes relevant to OCS leasing. See Wat! v. Energy 
Action Educ. Found., 454 U.S. 151 (1981); Secretary of the Interior 
v. California, 464 U.S. 312 (1984). 


20 


into contract relations, its rights and duties therein are 
governed generally by the law applicable to contracts be- 
tween private individuals.’” Wéinstar, 518 U.S. at 895 
(quoting Lynch v. United States, 292 U.S. 571, 579 
(1934)) (plurality opinion). See also id. at 911 (Breyer, 
J., concurring) (same); id. at 919-24 (Scalia, J., con- 
curring in judgment) (same). Accord Clearfield Trust 
Co. v. United States, 318 U.S. 363, 369 (1943) (“ {t]he 
United States does business on business terms’”) (quoting 
National Exch. Bank, 270 U.S. at 534); Perry v. United 
States, 294 U.S. 330, 352 (1935) (“When the United 
States, with constitutional authority, makes contracts, it 
has rights and incurs responsibilities similar to those of 
individuals who are parties to such instruments. There 
is no difference . . . except that the United States cannot 
be sued without its consent.”) (citation omitted); United 
States v. Bostwick, 94 U.S.. 53, 66 (1876) (same); 
Cooke v. United States, 91 U.S. 389, 398 (1875) (same). 


One fundamental principle of the “law applicable to 
contracts between private individuals” is that when a 
party’s performance of its obligations under a contract is 
made impossible by supervening events, the contract may 
be rescinded and the parties returned to the status quo 
ante by restitution of the consideration paid. “[{A] party 
who is excused from performance by snpervening impossi- 
bility is not privileged to keep something for nothing, and 
restitution is an available remedy against him.” 5 Corbin, 
supra, § 1102, at 549 (1964). Accord Restatement (Sec- 
ond) of Contracts § 377 & cmt. a (1981) (restitution 
appropriate where performance excused by supervening 
impossibility); id. § 264 (restitution appropriate when 
government regulation renders performance impossible): 
Restatement of Restitution § 108(c) (1937) (same): 18 
Walter H.E. Jaeger, Williston on Contracts § 1972, at 
224-27 (3d ed. 1978) (same). Thus, when performance 


21 


of a contract becomes impossible through no fault of 
either party, rescission of the contract and restitution of 
any consideration paid is an appropriate remedy. See, 
e.g., Winstar Corp. v. United States, 994 F.2d 797, 818-19 
(Fed. Cir. 1993) (Newman, J., dissenting), rev'd on 
reh'g, 64 F.3d 1531 (Fed. Cir. 1995) (en banc), aff'd, 
518 U.S. 839 (1996); Bishop Trust Co. Ltd. v. Kamokila 
Dev. Corp. v. Askew, 453 F.2d 819, 831 (Sth Cir. 1971). 


Contrary to this well-settled rule, the Federal Circuit 
held below that “the inability of a lessee to explore, if not 
attributable to the Government, does not create an en- 
titlement to any refund of the consideration paid to obtain 
the lease.” App. 18a. Even accepting arguendo the Fed- 
eral Circuit’s underlying assumption that Congress’s enact- 
ment of the OBPA “essentially had no effect,” id. at 14a, 
and that the “real reason” that the Secretary did not 
approve the Manteo Unit's POE was North Carolina’s 
CZMA objection as correct—which it is not °—petitioner 
nevertheless was entitled to restitution. In this case, the 
Secretary failed to perform his contractual duty to “timely 


®The Federal Circuit’s conclusion that the cause of the United 
States’ failure to approve the Manteo Unit’s POE was North Caro- 
lina’s CZMA objection is both factually and legally incorrect. First, 
the DOI’s stated reason for refusing to approve the Manteo Unit's 
POE in September 1990—i.e., within the 30-day period prescribed by 
the OCSLA, 43 U.S.C. § 1340(c)(1)—-was the need to comply with 
the OBPA. See App. 194a. North Carolina’s CZMA objection was 
credited to the DOI’s own explanation of its decision, and the 
government’s post hoc attempt to excuse the breach compelled by 
the OBPA must be rejected. Cf. SEC v. Chenery Corp., 332 U.S. 
194, 196 (1947) (reviewing court “must judge the propriety of 
[the agency’s] action solely by the grounds invoked by the 
agency”). Indeed, North Carolina did not even register its CZMA 
objection to the Manteo Unit’s POE until nearly two months after 
the date that DOI would, by its own admission, have been required 
to approve the POE, but for the OBPA. Finally, a State’s CZMA 
objection is not under the OCSLA a ground for withholding ap- 
proval of a POE. See supra at 5-6. 


22 


and fairly consider—not necessarily approve, but at least 
consider”"—the Manteo Unit’s POE, which the DOI had 
found to be “approvable in all respects.” Even if North 
Carolina’s CZMA objection—an event outside petition- 
er’s control—is viewed as a “supervening event” that com- 
pelled the United States’ failure, established principles of 
contract law entitled petitioner to restitution. Indeed, as 
Judge Newman observed, “[i]t is hornbook law that when 
a contract becomes impossible of performance through 
events outside the control of a party, restitution is an ap- 
propriate remedy.” /d. at 22a. 


Settled law also requires restitution in this case even if 
North Carolina’s intractable CZMA objections are viewed 
as a barrier to petitioner’s taking actions necessary to reap 
the benefits of its contract with the United States and 
its failure to overcome those objections as a failure on 
its part to “perform.” It is black-letter law that a plain- 
tiff seeking restitution (as opposed to other forms of re- 
lief) need not prove that, even if the defendant had not 
breached, he would have been able to perform his own 
obligations and receive the benefits of his contract. 


10 Relying on § 10 of the lease agreements—which provided that 
petitioner “shall comply with all regulations and orders relating to 
exploration, development, and production,’” App. 178a—the Federal! 
Circuit attempted to side-step petitioner’s claim for restitution by 
suggesting that it was petitioner (rather than the United States) 
that had failed to perform its obligation under its leases. See id. 
at 15a. However, the only “performance” required of petitioner 
under its OCS leases was paying up-front, cash “bonuses” of more 
than $78 million, making annual rental payments, and—if produc- 
tion ever began—paying royalties to the government. See id. at 
174a; see also id. at 176a-178a (§§ 4-7). Petitioner was not re- 
quired to submit POEs or PODs for any of its tracts, and in fact 
did not do so for its one non-Manteo tract. Moreover, a failure to 
secure a State’s CZMA concurrence is not a failure to “comply” 
with the OCSLA or a breach of an OCS lease; it is merely grounds 
for the Secretary to require modification of a particular POE, or 
to deny a particular drilling permit or POD. 


23 


A contract . . . may be rescinded, and the purchase 
moneys paid in advance may be recovered, on the 
failure of the seller to perform, even though the 
purchaser could not have performed. In an action to 
rescind and recover payments made on account of the 
purchase price[,] it is enough to show a breach by 
the seller. . . . {W)hen the action is to enforce the 
contract or to recover damages . . . [plaintiff] must 
tender and prove his own readiness, willingness, and 
ability to perform. 


Weintraub v. Rungmar Realty Corp., 231 N.Y.S.2d 241, 
244 (N.Y. Sup. Ct. 1962) (emphasis added); see also 
id. (buyer entitled to restitution of down payment where 
seller was in breach, even though buyer could not have 
performed his obligation to pay balance of purchase price 
at closing). Accord 4 Corbin, supra, § 978, at 928; 
Bertrand v. Jones, 156 A.2d 161, 167-69 (N.J. Super. 
1959). In this case, the OBPA flatly prohibited the Sec- 
retary from performing his promise to “timely and fairly 
consider” the plans and applications (including the Man- 
teo Unit’s POE) necessary for petitioner to exploit any 
of its contractual rights to explore and develop any of its 
OCS leases (including its one non-Manteo tract). The 
United States was thus in breach, see infra at 24-26, and 
it was irrelevant to petitioner’s claim for restitution (as 
opposed to other forms of relief which it did not seek) 
that it could not secure North Carolina’s CZMA con- 
currence. In short, even if petitioner’s duties under the 
leases included overcoming North Carolina’s obdurate 
opposition to drilling off its coast, neither party to the 
lease agreements was able to perform, and the result 
required was restitution by the government, not “the fed- 
eral government’s confiscation of Marathon’s payment[s].” 
App. 20a-21a (Newman, J., dissenting). The Federal 
Circuit’s decision to the contrary thus conflicted with this 
Court’s repeated holdings that government contracts are 


24 


governed by “the law applicable to contracts between 
private individuals,” Lynch v. United States, 292 U.S. 
571, 579 (1934). 


2. The Federal Circuit’s decision also conflicts with 
the well-established principle of contract law, which was 
recently reaffirmed by this Court in Winstar, that the 
government is liable for breach of contract when, by its 
own legislative acts, it makes performance of its contrac- 
tual obligations impossible. See, e.g., Far West Fed. Bank 
Vv. Office of Thrift Supervision-Director, 119 F.3d 1358, 
1364-65 (9th Cir. 1997); Stone Forest Indus., Inc. v. 
United States, 973 F.2d 1548, 1552 (Fed. Cir. 1992). 
See also E.H. Boly & Son, Inc. v. Schneider, 525 F.2d 
20, 23-24 (9th Cir. 1975). In Winstar, the United States 
had entered into contracts in which it specifically prom- 
ised to permit certain thrifts to use special accounting 
methods in exchange for their acquiring failing thrifts. 
518 U.S. at 867-68. Thereafter, Congress enacted legis- 
lation requiring federal regulators to prohibit them from 
using those accounting methods. /d. at 868-69. This 
Court held that, because Congress had made it impos- 
sible for the government to perform its promise under 
the contracts, it was in breach. See id. at 870 (plurality 
opinion); id. at 918 (Breyer, J., concurring); id. at 919 
(Scalia, J., concurring in judgment). 


Under Winstar, the OBPA plainly placed the United 
States in breach of its lease agreements with petitioner. 
Although the government had, in those lease agreements, 
promised to “timely and fairly consider” petitioner’s plans 
and permit applications, the OBPA made its performance 
of that promise impossible. The OBPA first imposed an 
absolute, 13-month ban on the Secretary’s performance 
of his duties. Thereafter, it unilaterally imposed onerous 
and unanticipated conditions on the Secretary’s perform- 
ance—conditions which were not satisfied with respect to 


5 


the Manteo Unit's first POE until April 1992, and which 
were never satisfied with respect to any other plan or 
application that petitioner was entitled to submit. See, 
e.g., Placid Oil Co. v. Humphrey, 244 F.2d 184, 188-89 
(Sth Cir. 1957) (lessor’s imposition of burdensome new 
conditions on oil lessee was breach of lease contract); 
United States v. Western Cas. and Sur. Co., 498 F.2d 
335, 339 (9th Cir. 1974) (unreasonable delay and im- 
position of new and onerous conditions on plaintiff con- 
stitutes breach); VunHaaren vy. State Farm Mut. Auto. 
Ins. Co., 989 F.2d 1, 6 (1st Cir. 1993) (same). 


As the Court of Federal Claims reasoned, the govern- 
ment’s OBPA-compelled failure timely to approve the 
Manteo Unit’s POE in September 1990—when no CZMA 
objection to that POE had been filed—was an actual 
breach of the lease agreements. See App. 76a. More- 
over, the enactment of OBPA’s bar on Secretarial approval 
for any other POE or POD that petitioner might submit 
(including ones for its non-Manteo tract)—a bar which 
was not lifted until the OBPA was repealed four years 
after this suit was filed—was an anticipatory breach. See 
id.; see also id. at 78a (“Since defendant is now legisla- 
tively barred from performing, it is in breach. It is not 
necessary for plaintiffs to have actually submitted such 
exploration plans because it is clear defendant cannot 
and will not perform its end of the bargain as required by 
the OCSLA.”). 


The Federal Circuit’s majority did not dispute (or even 
address) the Court of Federal Claims’ holding that the 
enactment of the OBPA placed the government in “total 
breach” of petitioner’s OCS leases by disabling it from 
approving any plans or applications that petitioner might 
submit. Instead, through judicial sleight of hand, the 
Federal Circuit mischaracterized petitioner’s claim as re- 


26 


lating solely to the Secretary's refusal to act on the single 
POE submitted by the Manteo Unit, and then dismissed 
that claim by erroneously finding that the OBPA was not 
the “real reason” for the Secretary’s failure to act. See 
App. 2la. (Newman, J., dissenting) (“The panel major- 
ity appears to misunderstand Marathon’s argument.”). In 
so doing, the Federal Circuit ignored this Court’s recent 
reaffirmation of the settled rule that when, through post- 
contract legislation, the government makes performance 
of its contractual obligations impossible in every instance 
in which its performance would be required, it places 
itself in total breach of its contract. See Winstar, 518 
U.S. at 870 (“When the law as to capital requirements 
changed in the present instance, the Government was 
unable to perform its promise and, therefore, became 
liable for breach.”) (plurality opinion). See also Far 
West Fed. Bank, 119 F.3d at 1365-66 (awarding restitu- 
tion where post-contract federal legislation prohibited the 
government from performing its contractual duties). In- 
stead, the Federal Circuit crafted a rule—applicable by 
its terms only to the government—that such a “total 
breach” may be excused where an alternative reason for 
the government's failure to perform in one instance may 
be found. The Federal Circuit's decision thus conflicts 
with this Court’s precedents and warrants review. 


Il. THE DECISION BELOW INVOLVES MATTERS OF 
OVERRIDING NATIONAL IMPORTANCE THAT 
WARRANT REVIEW NOW TO AVOID WIDE- 
SPREAD DISRUPTION AND UNCERTAINTY IN 
GOVERNMENT CONTRACTING. 


The Federal Circuit’s decision in this case creates pro- 
found uncertainty as to when the remedies of rescission 
and restitution are available against the federal govern- 
ment and calls into question the fundamental presumption 
of government contracting that “(t]he United States does 


27 


business on business terms,” United States v. National 
Exch. Bank, 270 U.S. 527, 534 (1926). Under the 
ordinary rules of contracts, a private oil lease would be 
ised, for example, to honor reasonable requests by the 
lessee to enter the lease tract to explore for oil, but was 
thereafter prohibited from performing his promise by sub- 
sequent government action by a State. See Restatement 
(Second) of Contracts §§ 264, 377 (1981). And, restitu- 
tion would clearly be available when the lessor, through 
his own acts—for example, adopting a rule prohibiting his 
employees from processing the lessee’s requests—made 
his own performance impossible. See, e.g., E.H. Boly & 
Son, Inc. v. Schneider, 525 F.2d 20, 23-24 (9th Cir. 
1975). 


Nonetheless, the Federal Circuit in this case carved a 
special exception for the government from the well- 
settled rules governing rescission and restitution by equat- 
ing events making the government's performance of its 
end of a bargain impossible with events—such as “impos- 
sible weather, equipment failures, [and] financial set- 
backs,” App. 18a—which merely make the fully per- 
formed bargain less valuable for the government’s con- 
tracting partner. The Federal Circuit’s decision thus casts 
into doubt one of the basic legal presumptions underlying 
not only the thousands of currently outstanding OCS 
leases," but also untold billions of dollars of other gov- 
ernment contracts. Under the Federal Circuit’s decision, 
it is unclear even whether a government contractor who 


11 At the end of 1997, there were 7,359 OCS leases outstanding. 
See Minerals Management Serv., U.S. Dep’t of the Interior, Mineral 
Revenues 1997, at 34 (1998). In addition, during 1998 OCS lessees 
paid $1.32 billion in up-front bonuses for 1,157 new OCS leases. 
See Minerals Management Serv., U.S. Dep’t of the Interior, Mineral 
Revenues 1998, at 51 (prelim. ed. 1999). 


28 


had delivered valuable goods to the federal government, 
and thereafter was denied payment because of a con- 
gressional appropriations embargo, would be entitled to 
the ordinary restitutionary remedy of simply getting his 
goods back. 

The consequences of the uncertainty created by the 
Federal Circuit's decision will be drastic. As the Court of 
Federal Claims observed, “common sense suggests that 
no sophisticated oil and gas company with many years 
of experience in drilling for oil in offshore leased tracts 
would knowingly agree to pay the huge, up-front con- 
siderations here involved,” App. 68a, without being able 
to predict whether, and under what circumstances, it could 
seek rescission and restitution if federal legislation later 
barred the United States from performing its end of the 
bargain. Likewise, no rational government contractor will 
comfortably deal with the government without the assur- 
ance that the common-law rules regarding rescission and 
restitution “applicable to contracts between private indi- 
viduals,” Lynch v. United States, 292 U.S. 571, 579 
(1934), bind the government as well. This Court should 
accordingly grant review in this case to resolve the ques- 
tion whether the United States, after having accepted con- 
sideration from a government contractor, is required to 
return that consideration when subsequent government 
action bars it from performing its contractual obligations. 


29 


CONCLUSION 


For the reasons stated above, this Court should grant 
the petition for a writ of certiorari.
